=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
In the nation of AtCoder, there are N cities numbered 1 to N, and M roads numbered 1 to M.
Road i connects cities A_i and B_i bidirectionally and has a length of C_i.
You are given Q queries to process in order. The queries are of the following two types.

- 1 i: Road i becomes closed.
- 2 x y: Print the shortest distance from city x to city y, using only roads that are not closed. If city y cannot be reached from city x, print -1 instead.

It is guaranteed that each test case contains at most 300 queries of the first type.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M Q
A_1 B_1 C_1
\vdots
A_M B_M C_M
\mathrm{query}_1
\vdots
\mathrm{query}_Q

Each query is in one of the following two formats:
1 i

2 x y

Output

Process the queries in order.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 300
- 0 \leq M \leq \frac{N(N-1)}{2}
- 1 \leq A_i < B_i \leq N
- All pairs (A_i, B_i) are distinct.
- 1 \leq C_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq Q \leq 2 \times 10^5
- In the queries of the first type, 1 \leq i \leq M.
- The road given in a query of the first type is not already closed at that time.
- The number of queries of the first type is at most 300.
- In the queries of the second type, 1 \leq x < y \leq N.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 3 5
1 2 5
1 3 10
2 3 6
2 1 3
1 2
2 1 3
1 1
2 1 3

Sample Output 1

10
11
-1


- In the first query, print the shortest distance from city 1 to city 3, which is 10.
- In the second query, road 2 becomes closed.
- In the third query, print the shortest distance from city 1 to city 3, which is 11.
- In the fourth query, road 1 becomes closed.
- In the fifth query, city 3 cannot be reached from city 1, so print -1.

Sample Input 2

4 6 6
2 3 1
2 4 1
3 4 1
1 2 1
1 3 1
1 4 1
1 4
1 5
1 6
2 1 2
2 1 3
2 1 4

Sample Output 2

-1
-1
-1

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

This uses old iu, iv, old du_old/dv_old. Is it sufficient? It computes paths that use new edge exactly once, with old shortest subpaths. Since any shortest path using new edge at most once can be decomposed as i->u (old) + edge + v->j (old) or i->v + edge + u->j (old). But wait prefix i->u in old graph might itself be improved by the new edge? Similarly suffix old. So yes, old distances suffice. Thus using old du/dv and old iu/iv is correct. Then update row i in-place. But when i later? We use old du/dv copies, so row u/v updates don't affect formula for later rows. Good. This is robust.

But is one pass over all i,j with old du/dv and old iu/iv enough? It computes all paths with exactly one new edge and old subpaths. The new shortest path after insertion either doesn't use new edge (old dist) or uses it once (old subpaths). So yes. Since edge nonnegative, no shortest path uses it more than once. Thus correct.

Input parsing:
import sys
def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it); M = next(it); Q = next(it)
    A=[0]*(M+1); B=[0]*(M+1); C=[0]*(M+1)
    for i in range(1,M+1):
        A[i]=next(it)-1; B[i]=next(it)-1; C[i]=next(it)
    closed=[False]*(M+1)
    queries=[]
    for _ in range(Q):
        t=next(it)
        if t==1:
            i=next(it)
            queries.append((1,i))
            closed[i]=True
        else:
            x=next(it)-1; y=next(it)-1
            queries.append((2,x,y))
    INF=10**30
    dist=[[INF]*N for _ in range(N)]
    for i in range(N):
        dist[i][i]=0
    for i in range(1,M+1):
        if not closed[i]:
            a=A[i]; b=B[i]; c=C[i]
            if c < dist[a][b]:
                dist[a][b]=c; dist[b][a]=c
    # Floyd
    for k in range(N):
        dk=dist[k]
        for i in range(N):
            dik=dist[i][k]
            if dik == INF: continue
            di=dist[i]
            nd_base=dik
            # local dk
            for j in range(N):
                nd = nd_base + dk[j]
                if nd < di[j]:
                    di[j]=nd
    ans=[]
    # process reverse

We can skip if iu >= INF and iv >= INF? But INF is 1e30; if iu=INF, iu+w+... > INF, not < di[j] (di[j] <= INF). So no effect. If one finite, update. Use if iu < INF or iv < INF? But INF + finite maybe > INF, not less. So if both INF, skip row. However, after insertion, if u/v unreachable from i, no path via edge. So skip.
                if iu == INF and iv == INF:
                    continue
                # But if iu=INF and iv finite, only second formula matters; if iu finite and iv INF, only first. We can branch to reduce.
                if iu < INF:
                    # update with du? Actually first formula uses dv_old
                    # for j: val = iu + w + dv_old[j]
                if iv < INF:
                    # second formula uses du_old
                # Need update di[j] for all j. Could combine loops? Branching inside j expensive.
                # Simpler no skip? 300*300*300 =27M, fine. But INF additions maybe okay. But if many unreachable, skip row saves.

            # But note if iu finite, we need update di[j] for all j using dv_old. If iv finite, update using du_old. If both finite, need both. We can do:

                base = iu + w
                dv = dv_old
                for j in range(N):
                    val = base + dv[j]
                    if val < di[j]: di[j] = val

Now, is the incremental update formula with old du/dv enough when adding edge to graph with existing distances? Let's prove more formally. Let G be old graph, D old all-pairs shortest distances. Add edge e=(u,v) weight w. Let D' be new distances. For any pair s,t, any shortest path P in G+e:
- If P doesn't use e, length >= D[s][t].
- If P uses e, because weights nonnegative, there is a shortest path using e at most once. If it uses e once, it consists of an s-u path in G, e, and v-t path in G (or s-v, e, u-t). The lengths of these subpaths are at least D[s][u], D[v][t] etc. Therefore D'[s][t] >= min(D[s][t], D[s][u]+w+D[v][t], D[s][v]+w+D[u][t])? Wait subpaths in G may not be shortest in G, but shortest old distances <= subpath lengths, so the expression with D is <= path length. Need show D'[s][t] <= expression by constructing paths: D[s][u] path in G, edge, D[v][t] path in G is valid in G+e, so D' <= expression. Thus equality. Good. So formula with old D exactly. Great.

Now, in our update loop, for row i, we compute iu = D[i][u], iv = D[i][v] old. But wait if i==u, iu=0; if i==v, iv=0. We copy du_old, dv_old before loop. For row i, di[u] and di[v] are old because row i hasn't been modified yet. Is that true for all i? We process rows in increasing i. Row i could have been modified when processing some previous row? No, previous row k modifies dist[k][j], not dist[i][j]. So row i unchanged. So yes. But what about if previous row k updated dist[k][u] and dist[k][v], not row i. So okay. Thus iu/iv old. Good.

But after processing row i, we update di[j]. For later row i+1, we don't use di. So okay.

Potential issue: We copy du_old = dist[u][:] and dv_old = dist[v][:] before loop. But if u or v row gets updated during loop when i=u or i=v, the copy remains old. Good. But what if u/v row is updated earlier by some previous row? Previous row k updates dist[k][j], not row u. So row u old until i=u. So copy at start is old. Good.

Could we avoid copying full rows by storing du_old = dist[u][:] and dv_old = dist[v][:] (N each) per insertion. K<=300, memory/time okay. Copy 300*300*300? Actually per insertion copy 2*N=600, K=300 => 180k, negligible.

Now, we need consider if adding edge with weight w but there is already a path between u,v with shorter distance. Then du_old[v] <= w maybe. The update formula still works; it may not change. If du_old[v] < w, then adding edge no improvement? Actually adding an edge that is not the shortest u-v path may still improve some distances? If there is already a path u-v of length < w, then any path using new edge can be replaced by old path between u,v, so no improvement. Formula will not improve because D[s][u]+w+D[v][t] >= D[s][u]+D[u][v]+D[v][t] >= D[s][t] by triangle. So okay.

For row u, du old (since not processed), dv old (since v not processed if we also postpone v). For row v, du old (u not processed), dv old. So no copy needed. But is it okay to process row u/v last? Yes, the update formula for row u/v uses old du/dv. But what about if row u's new distances could help row i via a path using new edge twice? Not needed. So we can update row u/v last. This avoids copying rows and ensures old rows. However, when processing row i not u/v, we update di[j] for all j, including j=u/v. That changes dist[i][u] and dist[i][v] but not du/dv. Good.

But there is a subtlety: When processing row i not u/v, we use iu = dist[i][u], iv = dist[i][v]. These are old because row i hasn't been processed yet. But could row i have been modified when processing previous row k? No. So okay. But what if previous row k was u or v? We postpone u/v, so no. Thus all rows not u/v processed before u/v, and row i not modified before. Good.

Then after processing all other rows, process row u:
- Need old du = dist[u] (unchanged), old dv = dist[v] (unchanged).
- For row u, iu = dist[u][u] = 0, iv = dist[u][v] old (unchanged). Update row u using du/dv old.
Then process row v:
- Need old du = dist[u]? But row u just updated, so dist[u] is no longer old. Need old du for row v formula? For row v, formula uses du_old (old row u) and dv_old (old row v). If we update row u before row v, dist[u] is new, not old. We could use a saved old row u or process row v before row u? If process row v before row u, dist[v] old, dist[u] old. Then update row v, then row u needs old dv, but dist[v] now new. Need save old row v. So to avoid copies, we need process one of u/v first and save the other row? Let's examine.

If we process all rows except u,v first, then both du,dv old. To update row u, we need du_old (row u itself old) and dv_old (row v old). If we update row u first, row u becomes new; then updating row v needs du_old (old row u) and dv_old (row v itself old). We no longer have old row u unless saved. Could instead update row v first, then row u needs old row v saved. So one copy needed for the row updated first? Actually if we update row v first, we need old row v (itself) and old row u. After updating row v, row v new; row u old. To update row u, need old row v, so save old row v. So one row copy. Alternatively, update row u and v simultaneously? We can compute new rows using old du/dv and assign? But row u/v updates also depend on old du/dv, not on each other's new values. We can compute new_u[j] and new_v[j] from old du/dv and old dist[u][v]? Let's see:
new_u[j] = min(old_u[j], old_u[u]+w+old_v[j], old_u[v]+w+old_u[j]) = min(old_u[j], w+old_v[j], old_u[v]+w+old_u[j]).
new_v[j] = min(old_v[j], old_v[u]+w+old_v[j], old_v[v]+w+old_u[j]) = min(old_v[j], old_v[u]+w+old_v[j], w+old_u[j]).
We could compute both rows in one pass and assign after, no copies? Need old_u and old_v lists; if we assign new_u in place, old_u lost for new_v. But new_v formula uses old_u[j] and old_v[j]. We can compute new_v first? Let's see:
For j, new_u_j = min(old_u[j], w+old_v[j], old_u[v]+w+old_u[j])
new_v_j = min(old_v[j], old_v[u]+w+old_v[j], w+old_u[j])
These are independent per j except old_u[v] and old_v[u] constants. We can compute both new_u[j] and new_v[j] for each j using old rows, then assign. We can do:

For edge (u,v,w):
1. Process all rows i not u and v:
   for i in range(N):
       if i == u or i == v: continue

Pseudo for insertion:
def add_edge(u,v,w):
    # update all rows except u,v
    du = dist[u]
    dv = dist[v]
    for i in range(N):
        if i == u or i == v:
            continue
        di = dist[i]
        iu = di[u]
        iv = di[v]
        if iu >= INF and iv >= INF:
            continue
        if iu < INF:
            base = iu + w
            # use dv
            for j in range(N):
                val = base + dv[j]
                if val < di[j]:
                    di[j] = val
        if iv < INF:
            base = iv + w
            # use du
            for j in range(N):
                val = base + du[j]
                if val < di[j]:
                    di[j] = val
    # update row u and row v
    old_u = du  # still old
    old_v = dv  # still old
    uv = old_u[v]
    vu = old_v[u]  # uv == vu
    # build new_u, new_v
    new_u = old_u[:]  # copy
    new_v = old_v[:]
    # For j:
    for j in range(N):
        # row u
        val = w + old_v[j]
        if val < new_u[j]:
            new_u[j] = val
        val = uv + w + old_u[j]
        if val < new_u[j]:
            new_u[j] = val
        # row v
        val = vu + w + old_v[j]
        if val < new_v[j]:
            new_v[j] = val
        val = w + old_u[j]
        if val < new_v[j]:
            new_v[j] = val
    dist[u] = new_u
    dist[v] = new_v

But wait: In step 1, for row i not u/v, we use du and dv references. If later in step 2 we assign dist[u]=new_u, du reference still old. Fine.

But there is a potential issue: In step 1, when processing row i, we update di[j] for all j. This includes j = u and j = v. For a later row k, we compute ku = dist[k][u], kv = dist[k][v]. Row k hasn't been modified, so okay. But what about if row k was modified as di? No, only current row modified. So okay.

Now, is the formula for row u/v using old_u/old_v complete? Let's test with simple graph where adding edge improves row u via path that uses old path from u to v plus edge v-u? That's val2. Good.

But wait, for row u, general formula also includes D[u][u]+w+D[v][j] = w+old_v[j], and D[u][v]+w+D[u][j] = uv+w+old_u[j]. But what about D'[u][j] could be D'[u][v] + w + D'[u][j]? That's self-referential; old formula enough. Good.

Now, could row u/v new distances be further improved by paths that go from u to some other row i (updated in step 1) and then use edge? For example, new path u -> a -> ... -> v -> edge -> u -> j? That uses edge maybe once? Wait if it goes u to v (old path), edge v-u, then u to j old. That's captured by uv+w+old_u[j]. If it goes u to a (old), a to v (old), edge v-u, then u to j old, same. If it goes u to a where a's distance to v improved by new edge? That would use edge twice. Not needed. So okay.

def add_edge(u, v, w):
    du_old = dist[u][:]
    dv_old = dist[v][:]
    for i in range_N:
        di = dist[i]
        iu = di[u]
        iv = di[v]
        if iu == INF and iv == INF:
            continue
        if iu < INF:
            if iv < INF:
                base1 = iu + w
                base2 = iv + w
                for j in range_N:
                    val = base1 + dv_old[j]
                    if val < di[j]:
                        di[j] = val
                    val = base2 + du_old[j]
                    if val < di[j]:
                        di[j] = val
            else:
                base = iu + w
                for j in range_N:
                    val = base + dv_old[j]
                    if val < di[j]:
                        di[j] = val
        else: # iv < INF
            base = iv + w
            for j in range_N:
                val = base + du_old[j]
                if val < di[j]:
                    di[j] = val

i=0: iu=0, iv=11 -> base1=10, base2=21. j=1 val1=10+dv_old[1]=16 >5; val2=21+du_old[1]=26; j=2 val1=10+0=10 <11 update row0[2]=10. Good.
i=1: row1 [5,0,6], iu=5, iv=6 -> base1=15, base2=16. j=0 val1=15+11=26 >5; val2=16+0=16 >5; j=2 val1=15+0=15 >6; val2=16+5=21. no.
i=2: row2 [11,6,0], iu=11, iv=0 -> base1=21, base2=10. j=0 val2=10+0=10 <11 update row2[0]=10; j=1 val2=10+5=15 >6. Good.
q (2,0,2): d=10 -> ans 10. Reverse ans [ -1,11,10], reverse -> 10,11,-1. Good.

Now, let's maybe write final code:

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    p = 0
    N = data[p]; M = data[p+1]; Q = data[p+2]; p += 3
    A = [0]*(M+1)
    B = [0]*(M+1)
    C = [0]*(M+1)
    for i in range(1, M+1):
        A[i] = data[p] - 1
        B[i] = data[p+1] - 1
        C[i] = data[p+2]
        p += 3
    closed = [False]*(M+1)
    queries = []
    type2_count = 0
    for _ in range(Q):
        t = data[p]; p += 1
        if t == 1:
            i = data[p]; p += 1
            queries.append((1, i))
            closed[i] = True
        else:
            x = data[p] - 1
            y = data[p+1] - 1
            p += 2
            queries.append((2, x, y))
            type2_count += 1
    del data
    if type2_count == 0:
        return
    INF = 10**30
    dist = [[INF]*N for _ in range(N)]
    for i in range(N):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    for i in range(1, M+1):
        if not closed[i]:
            a = A[i]; b = B[i]; c = C[i]
            if c < dist[a][b]:
                dist[a][b] = c
                dist[b][a] = c
    range_N = range(N)
    # Floyd-Warshall
    for k in range_N:
        dk = dist[k]
        for i in range_N:
            di = dist[i]
            dik = di[k]
            if dik == INF:
                continue
            for j in range_N:
                nd = dik + dk[j]
                if nd < di[j]:
                    di[j] = nd
    ans = []
    for q in reversed(queries):
        if q[0] == 2:
            _, x, y = q
            d = dist[x][y]
            if d < INF:
                ans.append(str(d))
            else:
                ans.append("-1")
        else:
            _, idx = q
            u = A[idx]; v = B[idx]; w = C[idx]
            du_old = dist[u][:]
            dv_old = dist[v][:]
            for i in range_N:
                di = dist[i]
                iu = di[u]
                iv = di[v]
                if iu == INF and iv == INF:
                    continue
                if iu < INF and iv < INF:
                    base1 = iu + w
                    base2 = iv + w
                    for j in range_N:
                        val = base1 + dv_old[j]
                        if val < di[j]:
                            di[j] = val
                        val = base2 + du_old[j]
                        if val < di[j]:
                            di[j] = val
                elif iu < INF:
                    base = iu + w
                    for j in range_N:
                        val = base + dv_old[j]
                        if val < di[j]:
                            di[j] = val
                else:  # iv < INF
                    base = iv + w
                    for j in range_N:
                        val = base + du_old[j]
                        if val < di[j]:
                            di[j] = val
    ans.reverse()
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's consider if we can optimize insertion update by not copying rows if `w` is not smaller than current dist[u][v]? Actually if adding edge is not the shortest u-v path, it cannot improve any distance, because any path using new edge can replace edge by old u-v path of length <= w, yielding a path in old graph no longer. Is that true? If old dist[u][v] <= w, then for any s,t, D[s][u]+w+D[v][t] >= D[s][u]+D[u][v]+D[v][t] >= D[s][t] by triangle. Similarly other orientation. So no improvement. We can skip insertion entirely if `dist[u][v] <= w`. This can save time if adding redundant edges. But in reverse, adding a road that was closed; if there is an alternative path shorter or equal, no distances change. We can check before copying/updating:
if w < dist[u][v]:
    # update
else:
    # no update

Suppose old dist[u][v] = w (same length but different path). Adding edge doesn't create shorter distances because any path using new edge can replace with old path of same length, resulting in a walk no longer. Since old graph already has a path of length w, the new edge is redundant. So yes. This can significantly reduce work if many closed roads have alternative paths. But worst-case still K. Add this optimization:
if w < dist[u][v]:
    # perform insertion
else:
    # no change

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with `if w < dist[u][v]` skip optimization. Let's prove again with triangle. Suppose D[u][v] <= w. For any s,t, candidate via u->v: D[s][u] + w + D[v][t] >= D[s][u] + D[u][v] + D[v][t]. By triangle inequality for shortest paths, D[s][t] <= D[s][u] + D[u][v] + D[v][t] (path s->u, u->v, v->t is a walk in old graph, so shortest <= its length). Thus candidate >= D[s][t]. Similarly other orientation. So no improvement. Good. If D[u][v] == w, candidate >= D[s][t]. So skip. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with adding edge where `w < dist[u][v]` but `dist[u][v]` is not symmetric? Dist matrix should be symmetric because graph undirected. Floyd preserves symmetry. Insertion update should preserve symmetry. Let's check our insertion update with copies and row order preserves symmetry? It should. But let's verify: We update row i using old du/dv. For pair (i,j), update uses D[i][u]+w+D[v][j] and D[i][v]+w+D[u][j]. For pair (j,i), when row j processed, it will use D[j][u]+w+D[v][i] and D[j][v]+w+D[u][i]. Since old D symmetric, and updates symmetric? But row i update may set dist[i][j] to a value; row j update later may set dist[j][i] to same? Need ensure. The formula is symmetric in i,j if old D symmetric. But because row i processed before row j, row i uses old D[i][u], D[v][j], etc. Row j uses old D[j][u], D[v][i], etc. Old D symmetric, so D[j][u]=D[u][j], D[v][i]=D[i][v], etc. The candidate for j,i via u->v: D[j][u]+w+D[v][i] = D[u][j]+w+D[i][v], which is the second candidate for i,j via v->u? Wait for i,j candidates:
C1 = D[i][u]+w+D[v][j]
C2 = D[i][v]+w+D[u][j]
For j,i:
C1' = D[j][u]+w+D[v][i] = D[u][j]+w+D[i][v] = C2
C2' = D[j][v]+w+D[u][i] = D[v][j]+w+D[i][u] = C1
So the set of candidates is same. Thus if row i update sets dist[i][j] to min(old, C1, C2), row j update will set dist[j][i] to min(old, C1', C2') = same. Good. So symmetry preserved.

Now, let's think about if row i update uses `di[j]` after it may have been updated by first candidate; row j update uses old row j (not modified) because row j not processed yet. Good.

    for i in range_N:
        di = dist[i]
        dik = di[k]
        if dik == INF:
            continue
        for j in range_N:
            nd = dik + dk[j]
            if nd < di[j]:
                di[j] = nd

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "data" list and `del data`; if we use `data` in list comprehension? no.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "AtCoder" and PyPy recursion no.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "commit-implement-recheck" instruction; final code only. Good.

Now, maybe we should include comments in code for clarity? Not necessary but okay. The final
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    p = 0

    N = data[p]
    M = data[p + 1]
    Q = data[p + 2]
    p += 3

    A = [0] * (M + 1)
    B = [0] * (M + 1)
    C = [0] * (M + 1)

    for i in range(1, M + 1):
        A[i] = data[p] - 1
        B[i] = data[p + 1] - 1
        C[i] = data[p + 2]
        p += 3

    closed = [False] * (M + 1)
    queries = []
    type2_count = 0

    for _ in range(Q):
        t = data[p]
        p += 1
        if t == 1:
            i = data[p]
            p += 1
            queries.append((1, i))
            closed[i] = True
        else:
            x = data[p] - 1
            y = data[p + 1] - 1
            p += 2
            queries.append((2, x, y))
            type2_count += 1

    del data

    if type2_count == 0:
        return

    INF = 10 ** 30
    dist = [[INF] * N for _ in range(N)]
    for i in range(N):
        dist[i][i] = 0

    for i in range(1, M + 1):
        if not closed[i]:
            a = A[i]
            b = B[i]
            c = C[i]
            if c < dist[a][b]:
                dist[a][b] = c
                dist[b][a] = c

    range_N = range(N)

    # Floyd-Warshall for the graph after all closures
    for k in range_N:
        dk = dist[k]
        for i in range_N:
            di = dist[i]
            dik = di[k]
            if dik == INF:
                continue
            for j in range_N:
                nd = dik + dk[j]
                if nd < di[j]:
                    di[j] = nd

    ans = []

    # Process queries backwards, adding closed edges
    for q in reversed(queries):
        if q[0] == 2:
            _, x, y = q
            d = dist[x][y]
            ans.append(str(d) if d < INF else "-1")
        else:
            _, idx = q
            u = A[idx]
            v = B[idx]
            w = C[idx]

            # If an alternative path is already no longer than this edge,
            # adding this edge cannot improve any distance.
            if w >= dist[u][v]:
                continue

            du_old = dist[u][:]
            dv_old = dist[v][:]

            for i in range_N:
                di = dist[i]
                iu = di[u]
                iv = di[v]

                if iu == INF and iv == INF:
                    continue

                if iu < INF and iv < INF:
                    base1 = iu + w
                    base2 = iv + w
                    for j in range_N:
                        val = base1 + dv_old[j]
                        if val < di[j]:
                            di[j] = val
                        val = base2 + du_old[j]
                        if val < di[j]:
                            di[j] = val
                elif iu < INF:
                    base = iu + w
                    for j in range_N:
                        val = base + dv_old[j]
                        if val < di[j]:
                            di[j] = val
                else:
                    base = iv + w
                    for j in range_N:
                        val = base + du_old[j]
                        if val < di[j]:
                            di[j] = val

    ans.reverse()
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
